

===== PAGE 1 =====
A probabilistic model for the analysis of
modular structure in complex biological
networks
James Peter Gilbert
Thesis submitted to The University of
Nottingham
for the degree of Doctor of Philosophy
2015


===== PAGE 2 =====
Abstract
The notion of a modular, complex biological network is at the forefront of
research in systems biology. Much of the recent focus on module detection
has focused on developing new algorithms capable of detecting these biological
modules. However, little work has been conducted into evaluating which
algorithms researchers should select given in a practical context. Evaluating
the performance of algorithms in a practical context proves that algorithms lack
agreement in terms of the underlying modules detected. This thesis presents
a novel model for the generation of large scale, heterogeneous networks with
community structure that aims to mimic the topology found in real world
networks. Using a geometric approach to generating heterogeneous degree
distributions, assortative and disassortative networks with high clustering
coeﬃcients are generated through creating block structure. This model is then
ﬁtted to empirical data sets with a good degree of accuracy for target summary
statistics. This allows the deﬁnition of a methodology for algorithm selection in a
practical context, giving researchers an indication of the reliability of algorithms.
Given that a known modular structure is generated this allows the analysis
of algorithms, ﬁnding that assortative conﬁgurations and realistic topology
signiﬁcantly impact the performance of many module detection algorithms. The
underlying assumption that a module is indistinguishable from a non-modular
random graph provides the basis for potential work into modelling hierarchical
systems.

===== PAGE 13 =====
Chapter 1
Introduction
The detection of modules in complex biological networks is a considerable
challenge that relates to many areas of biological hypothesis generation. The
algorithms that perform these tasks are crucial to the development and un-
derstanding of the inner workings of living things. This thesis concerns the
development of tools to aid the analysis, evaluation and selection of module
detection algorithms in a practical context. By providing realistic, models
that ﬁt observed topological properties, it is possible to provide the conclusive
analysis of algorithmic performance. However, to date current modelling ap-
proaches lack key topological properties such as assortativity and, instead, focus
on general “universal” properties rather than providing domain and context
speciﬁc tasks. This introductory chapter aims to outline the motivation and
aims of the thesis and gives a broad outline of each of the chapters contained
within.
1.1 Background and motivation
Since the development of high throughput “omics” data collection method,
the biosciences have become deluged with big data problems that require the
development of new analysis methods [2]. Seeing the world through the eyes of
11

===== PAGE 14 =====
a “one gene one function” is a view of nature that is quickly being replaced
with a view of systemic function. At the core of the methodology of this
systems approach biology is the abstraction of a biological network [3]. This
approach focuses on understanding the function of genes and proteins through
their interactions with one another and the outside world.
This systems thinking approach has given rise to the notion of biological
modules; sub-networks of genes that perform speciﬁc, isolated functions that
relate to testable hypotheses [4]. These modules have been shown to relate
to known biological processes such as complexes within networks of protein
interactions [5].
The detection of these modular components borrows heavily from the
ﬁeld of complex networks. This discipline focuses on uncovering how large
scale topological properties inﬂuence systemic behaviour [6]. The ﬁeld has
been popularised by notions such as “small-worlds” [7], where networks are
characterised by shortest paths owning to, amongst other things, “scale-free” [8]
topology where extremely heterogeneous properties are found to emerge. This
popularity has crossed over in to computational biology in many core areas.
The development of module discovery in biological networks is then closely
related to the idea of community detection in sociological networks [9]. Con-
sequently, the words “community“ and “module“ are used interchangeably
throughout this thesis. This work promises interesting results, yet the recent
explosion in the number of methods at the researchers disposal [9] has created
its own set of problems. Few benchmarks exist to evaluate community detec-
tion algorithms [10], and those that do exist are often extremely general and
lack the ability to properly mimic the topology of other real world networks.
Fundamentally, however, the assumption behind what a community actually
should be deﬁned as focuses largely on detectability making the evaluation of
algorithms even more complicated.
12

===== PAGE 15 =====
1.2 Aims and objectives
The focus of this research is highly interdisciplinary, requiring the development of
theoretical methods with a view to improving biological research. This requires
the exploration of mechanisms for detecting biological modules, integrating
external data sources and providing tools for the visualisation of the data. This
is inherently exploratory in nature, seeking to explore the current methods
of validating module detection in large scale complex networks. Here, the
particular focus will be on the use of statistical techniques for validating
clusters against existing knowledge and experimental data sets.
The core objective of this thesis is the development of a methodology for
the selection and evaluation of module detection. In order to achieve this goal,
a number of key points need to be achieved:
• A model capable of generating synthetic complex networks with realistic
topology and a known community structure
• A formal assumption that deﬁnes what a community structure really is
• The ability to tune the parameters of this model to ﬁt desired degree
distributions, clustering and degree assortativity coeﬃcients
• The evaluation of the impact degree assortativity has upon community
detection algorithms
• A formal methodology for evaluating the performance of module detection
algorithms in the context of realistic models
1.3 Organisation of the thesis
This section outlines each chapter of this dissertation.
13

===== PAGE 16 =====
Chapter 3 provides the literature review for this thesis. This can be thought
of as being broadly broken into two sections, a review of relevant work with
regards to biological correlation of expression, protein interaction and metabolic
networks involved in this study. The second half of this section is concerned
with the theory and topology of complex networks in a wider sense. Particular
focus is paid to relevant models for the generation of topological structure as
well as a review of existing module detection algorithms used throughout this
study.
Chapter 4 then moves on to a core practical and theoretical application for
the theory of complex networks in the form of whole genome correlation of
expression data sets taken from plant biology. This serves as an evaluation of the
state of the art in module detection. Particular focus is paid to the limitation
of selecting such methods and ways to evaluate the detected communities
using available, externally curated meta-data. The ﬁnal section of this chapter
presents a web visualisation tool for these methods that oﬀers bioscientists the
ability to query the large scale datasets used in this study.
Chapter 5 introduces the Circular Gauassian Random Graph Model
(CiGRAM). This is an approach to generating synthetic networks with re-
alistic topology and community structure. CiGRAM is an extended form of
ﬁxed density random graphs that uses latent geometric variables to generate de-
gree correlations and heterogeneity, with block structure to form modules. This
approach takes a fundamental assumption that a module is indistinguishable
from a random sub-graph, providing an approach for evaluating community
detection algorithms.
In Chapter 6 the applicability of CiGRAM is validated. This comes in the
form of evaluating the spectral properties CiGRAM is capable of generating
as well as the use of spectral distance and summary statistics to ﬁt real world
networks.
Chapter 7 formally demonstrates how CiGRAM can be used to evaluate
14

===== PAGE 17 =====
community detection algorithms. One aspect of this work is the analysis of
community detection algorithms in the context of assortative graphs, a property
observed in the data sets evaluated in Chapter 4. The chapter then presents
a formal methodology for the evaluation of community detection algorithms
in the context of best ﬁt models from Chapter 6. This presents an approach
to algorithm evaluation and selection in a practical context. The chapter also
includes a comparison of CiGRAM to other related benchmark graphs.
The thesis then concludes in Chapter 8, providing a summary of the work
conducted. This also includes a discussion into how well the core aims and
objectives of this work were met as well a view to possible future directions of
this research. This section also brieﬂy discuses the publications that have been
written or are in the process of submission as a consequence of this thesis.
1.4 Contributions to knowledge
The research undertaken as part of this thesis has demonstrated applicability
of module detection algorithms to correlation of expression data sets. The use
of statistical methods to aid biological discovery gives several speciﬁc biological
hypothesis that can be experimentally validated, such as the relationship
between co-expressed biological modules and evolutionarily conserved genes.
This work also highlights a core limitation in the current methods due to the
lack of agreement between the diﬀerent module detection algorithms. The web
visualisations of the datasets used in Chapter 4 also provide the opportunity
for researchers to explore the large scale expression data sets with a view to
hypothesis generation. This is provided without the need to download and
conduct a lengthy analysis of the data, as other sources of information are
readily integrated into the tools.
The most signiﬁcant contribution of this thesis is CiGRAM. This model
generates realistic community structure through a simple assumption about
15

===== PAGE 18 =====
the deﬁnition of a module, that it is indistinguishable from a random graph in
terms of divisibility. This allowed the development of a methodology for the
evaluation of module detection algorithms in the context of realistic synthetic
models of datasets, through the exploration of procedures to ﬁt real world data.
It was discovered that certain key algorithms perform signiﬁcantly worse in the
context of high levels of degree assortativity, a property observed in empirical
data.
16

===== PAGE 19 =====
Chapter 2
Preliminary graph theory
This Chapter gives the basic deﬁnitions of terminology relating to graph theory
used throughout this thesis. Throughout this thesis we use the words “network”
and “graph” interchangeably. A network can refer to any set of objects, referred
to as nodes or vertices, that interact according to some speciﬁc pattern of edges.
This notion inherently relates to the ﬂow of information between objects in a
system. If one imagines a particle taking a random walk around a network,
stopping at each vertex, the set of vertices that can be visited is always
dependent on the set of adjacent edges at the current vertex. A graph can be
thought of as an intuitive map between related elements that could relate to
direct interactions, correlations, or the notion of a discrete state space in which
each node describes the current state of some system and any adjacent vertices
relate to the states that can be transitioned to. The concern of this thesis is
the topological and structural properties underlying biological networks.
2.1 Basic concepts
Formally, consider an undirected, unweighted graphG as a set of vertices V and
edges E such that a pair of vertices i and j are considered to be connected if
the tuple (i,j ) is present in the set of edges E. By convention we will term the
17

===== PAGE 20 =====
number of vertices in a graph as the cardinality of the vertex set n =|V| and
the number of edges as the cardinality of the edge set m =|E|. Simultaneously
we consider the adjacency matrix of a graph A to be the n×n binary matrix
representingG such that Aij = 1 if vertices i and j are adjacent and Aij = 0
otherwise. Where a graph has edges that are non-equivalent, we consider this
a weighted graph. In the case of a weighted graph, the elements of A can take
on any real number.
In the case of directed graphs, or digraphs, we consider A to be non-
symmetric and the order of ( i,j ) to be relevant. The direction of an edge
indicates the available ﬂow of information. If the link ( i,j ) is present within a
digraph then information can ﬂow from vertex i to vertexj, whilst if the vertex
(j,i ) is not present then no information can pass from j to i.
Node degree refers to the number of adjacent edges that a node has. We
can consider the total degree of a node to be ki =∑j∈V Aij or in set theoretic
notation as the cardinality of the set of edges containing i, ki =|{(i,j )|(i,j )∈
E}|. The set of edges adjacent to a given vertex is its neighbourhood. In the
case of directed graphs we can consider indegree, the cardinality of the set of
adjacent edges pointing to the node, and outdegree, the cardinality of the set
of adjacent edges pointing to other vertices.
The density graph is an important property and should be considered when
comparing graphs in the presence of topological measures or summary statistics.
We deﬁne the density of an undirected graph as,
d(G) = 2mn(n− 1). (2.1)
When d(G) is close to 1 the graph is considered dense, whereas graphs with
density close to 0 are considered sparse. A similar calculation to density is
the average node degree denoted by ˆk = 2mn . Note that a hard deﬁnition of
“sparseness” depends upon the cardinality of the vertex set, in this case the
values of the adjacency matrix are mostly zero. Almost all complex networks
18

===== PAGE 21 =====
are considered sparse, and structural properties such as heterogeneity in the
number of edges of each node often require sparse graphs [11].
A subgraph of a graph is any non-empty subset of the nodes and edges. A
path, or walk, within a graph is any ordered sequence of vertices such that an
edge exists between each vertex. The shortest path between two nodes is the
path with the lowest cardinality; many such paths may exist. A graph is said
to be connected if there exists a path between each pair of vertices. The largest
connected component of a graph is the largest connected subgraph. An induced
subgraph is a subset of the vertices such that any edges between those vertices
forms a connected graph. An n-clique refers to any fully connected graph or
subgraph that contains an edge between all pairs of vertices.
A cycle is any closed walk such that the path starts and ends on the same
node, with no repetitions of vertices in between. A tree is a form of acyclic
graph, that is to say that without cycles there is one and only one path between
each pair of vertices. Trees are both necessarily connected and contain exactly
n− 1 edges, adding any edge to a tree will, therefore, introduce a cycle.
We deﬁne a two star as a path containing any triple of nodes, of speciﬁc
interest is the number of two stars a node is central to. The number of two
stars a given node is central to can be deﬁned as,
si =ki(ki− 1). (2.2)
This is an important property when considering local and global network
statistics such as the clustering coeﬃcient [7], described in detail later.
Another important measure relating to networks is the mean shortest path
length. The mean shortest path length is given by,
l(G) = 1n ∑(i,j)∈V sp(i,j ), (2.3)
wheresp(i,j ) is the shortest path length between a pair of vertices. In Chapter
3 Section 3.3.2, the notion of a small world network is discussed. In this context,
the mean shortest path length is used to characterise a speciﬁc form of network.
19

===== PAGE 22 =====
2.2 Measures of topology
The following section summarises some of the important topological summary
measurements that are used to characterise networks in this thesis. The reader
is referred to a review [12] for a more comprehensive list of measurements.
2.2.1 Degree distributions
The degree distribution is an important summary statistic that will be discussed
at length in this thesis. For simple graphs, a histogram is suﬃcient to model
the degree distribution, selecting a number of bins appropriate to the network
size. However, many of the networks studied in this thesis follow heavy tailed
distributions, making it diﬃcult to select an appropriate number of bins [13].
Consequently, the convention of the complementary cumulative distribution
which considers the probability that you will ﬁnd a node with degree greater
than a given value P (x≤k). These are viewed on a log-log scale in order to
easily diﬀerentiate between distributions. Further information on heavy tailed
degree distributions is provided in Chapter 3 Section 3.3.1.
2.2.2 Clustering coeﬃcients
A triangle is deﬁned as the triple, ( i,j,k ), such that the three nodes form a
complete subgraph, sometimes termed a transitive closure. An elegant way
of measuring this transitivity is the clustering coeﬃcient [7]. The clustering
coeﬃcient of a node is given by,
Ci = 2ti
ki(ki− 1) (2.4)
where ti is the number of triangles containing node i. Where Ci = 1 we can
say that vertex i is in every possible triangle that it can be contained within,
likewise where ki = 0 we can see that i is contained within no triangles. This
deﬁnition of the clustering coeﬃcient is equivalent to the density of the induced
20

===== PAGE 23 =====
subgraph of a node’s neighbourhood. It is conventional to measure the mean
clustering for a given network C = 1N∑i∈V Ci against a random graph of
equivalent density. When considering the clustering coeﬃcient, the convention
used throughout this thesis is to ignore nodes with a degree of 1 as they are
not central to two stars.
2.2.3 Vertex criticality
The notion that a vertex is critical to the structure and function of a network
is captured by centrality measures [12]. The most commonly used measure
is betweenness centrality [14] which measures the fraction of shortest paths
through a given node as follows,
Bu =
∑
i,j∈V
δ(i,u,j )
δ(i,j ) , (2.5)
where δ(i,u,j ) is the number of shortest paths between vertices i and j that
pass through vertex u and δ(i,j ) is the total number of shortest paths between
i and j. The betweenness centrality calculation can also be applied to edges as
Equation 2.5 allows u to be an edge or a vertex. This is an approach used, for
example, in the Newman-Girvan algorithm for detecting modular structure [15].
In order to measure the dependence the graph has on a small number of
vertices, the central point dominance of a graph is deﬁned by,
CPD = 1n− 1∑i∈V Bmax−Bi, (2.6)
WhereBmax is the maximum betweenness over all vertices. CPD is necessarily
in the range [0, 1], a value of 0 indicates that the graph is highly decentralised
and not dependent upon any small number of vertices. In contrast, where
CPD is close to 1, the network is highly dependent upon a small number of
vertices with a high level of betweenness centrality.
21

===== PAGE 24 =====
2.3 Graph Laplacians
Spectral clustering is one of the oldest methods for partitioning graphs [16]
and refers to methods that partition data according to using the eigenvectors
of matrices. The approach taken with graphs is either to use the Laplacian
or Normalised Laplacian of the graph rather than the adjacency matrix. The
Laplacian of an adjacency matrix is given by,
L =D−A (2.7)
where D is deﬁned as the degree matrix of A, that is to say D is a diagonal
matrix such that Dii =ki, the degree of each vertex. We can then see that the
elements of L can be deﬁned as,
Lij =



ki if i =j,
−1 if i⁄=j and Aij = 1,
0 otherwise .
(2.8)
The normalised form of the Laplacian matrix is deﬁned by,
L =I−D− 12AD− 12, (2.9)
from this deﬁnition we can then see that the normalised Laplacian takes the
form [17],
Lij =

(2.10)
Conventionally, spectral methods use the eigenvectors of the graph as a pro-
jection of the graph onto a metric space. This allows one to use conventional
clustering approaches such as k-means clustering to partition the graph space.
However, many of the approaches used within this domain do not lend well to
22

===== PAGE 25 =====
complex, heterogeneous networks of the form we are interested in as they often
assume properties such as roughly uniform cluster size [18,19].
Whilst conventional spectral clustering is extremely limited in its application
to large, heterogeneous sparse graphs found here, Fay et al. [17] summarised
the potential as a form of graph distance measure using the eigenvalues of the
normalised Laplacian to characterise the structure of graphs. This approach is
of little help to ﬁnding modular structure directly, but may have implications
for ﬁnding appropriate models both with and without community structure.
In Chapter 6 these distances are applied to ﬁtting graphs models to empirical
data.
